## Page 1


NAKED SINGULARITIES BEYOND SPHERICAL SYMMETRY:
SINGULAR INNER CAUCHY HORIZONS
FOR THE EINSTEIN-SCALAR FIELD SYSTEM
XINLIANG AN AND SHENGRONG WU
Abstract. In this work, we investigate the formation of naked singularities for the
3 + 1-dimensional Einstein-scalar field system without symmetry assumptions. We gen-
eralize the spherically symmetric and self-similar naked-singularity solution constructed
by Christodoulou in [4] by prescribing non-spherically symmetric initial data along both
incoming and outgoing initial null hypersurfaces. We then establish global existence for
the resulting solutions and analyze the singular structure of the inner Cauchy horizon.
Our construction is based on employing a notion of four-type differences and design-
ing a system of scale-invariant weighted norms to control the corresponding geometry.
We show that the constructed spacetimes retain a global naked-singularity structure,
characterized by an incomplete future null infinity and a singular inner Cauchy horizon.
Moreover, we derive detailed asymptotics near the inner Cauchy horizon and prove the
desired C1,
κ
1−κ +inextendibility of these solutions, where κ ∈(0, 1/3) is the self-similar
parameter. This indicates a connection between weak and strong cosmic censorship: for
the class of non-spherically symmetric solutions constructed here, the failure of weak cos-
mic censorship in its strict formulation is accompanied by a quantitative inextendibility
mechanism at the inner Cauchy horizon.
1. Introduction
In this article, we study the Einstein-scalar field equations (ESE):



Ricµν(g) −1
2R(g)gµν = DµϕDνϕ −1
2DλϕDλϕgµν,
□gϕ = 0,
(1.1)
where (M, g) is a 3+1-dimensional Lorentzian manifold with Ricci curvature Ricµν(g) and
scalar curvature R(g), and ϕ : M →R is a real-valued scalar function.
In [4] Christodoulou constructed the celebrated naked singularity solution for this system.
This solution arises under the assumption of continuous self-similarity (CSS) and spherical
symmetry.
In renormalized double null coordinates, the background solution is solved
under the following ansatz:
Spherical Symmetry:
g = −2Ω2(u, v)(du ⊗dv + dv ⊗du) + r2(u, v)dσS2,
κ-Self-Similarity:
S = u∂u + v∂v,
LSg = 2g,
S(ϕ) =
√
2κ.
(1.2)
Both constructing more examples of naked singularity solutions beyond these symmetries
and proving the stability or instability of these naked singularities are challenging and are
closely tied to the weak cosmic censorship conjecture (WCCC), one of the central problems
in mathematical general relativity.
Conjecture 1.1 (Weak Cosmic Censorship, [9, 6]). The maximal future development of
generic, regular, asymptotically flat initial data for the Einstein field equations possesses a
complete future null infinity.

Key words and phrases. naked singularities, inner Cauchy horizons, cosmic censorship, Einstein-scalar
field equations, non-spherical symmetry.

## Page 2


2
XINLIANG AN AND SHENGRONG WU
For the system (1.1), under spherically symmetry, in a series of foundational works
[2, 3, 5] Christodoulou showed that all naked singularities are unstable, i.e., each naked
singularity initial datum is associated with two unstable directions and the generic per-
turbation leads to black hole formation.
Recently, the first author [1] proved that for
Christodoulou’s naked-singularity solution, even a small anisotropic perturbation can pro-
duce an anisotropic spacelike apparent horizon and thereby censors the singularities behind
the achronal dynamical horizon. In [1] it also shows that, for any k ∈Z+, Christodoulou’s
naked-singularity solution has at least codimensional 2k nonlinear instability under out-
going anisotropic characteristic perturbations. Here we also note the work [7] by Li-Liu
and their result shows that the spherically symmetric singularities considered in [7] are un-
stable (as a result of trapped surface formation) subject to close-to-isotropic gravitational
perturbations and the instability there is in the sense of first category.
These above instability results raise a complementary set of questions: Can naked sin-
gularities persist under a suitable class of non-spherically symmetric perturbations? If so,
since weak cosmic censorship in its strict formulation, without the genericity requirement,
appears to be violated, is there another censorship mechanism that takes effect?
In this
article, we prove that naked singularities form from non-spherically symmetric initial data
lying in a small neighborhood of Christodoulou’s self-similar solution [4]. We further show
that the singular boundary (the inner Cauchy horizon) of the corresponding spacetime ad-
mits no Hölder extension. For our initial data, we prescribe non-spherically symmetric
κ-self-similar perturbations of [4] on the initial incoming null hypersurface and small per-
turbations on the initial outgoing null hypersurface. In the setting considered there, we find
that when weak cosmic censorship fails in its strict formulation, strong cosmic censorship
comes into force.
Our results here also connect another direction. In the vacuum setting, Rodnianski and
Shlapentokh-Rothman [11] proved the formation of naked singularities from their intro-
duced κ-self similar initial data for the Einstein vacuum equations. Their result [11] gives
the first mathematical construction of naked-singularity solutions without symmetry as-
sumption, and their results and methods inspired the present paper. We also mention that
in the spherically symmetric Einstein-scalar field setting, Singh [12] proved the stability
of Christodoulou’s solution under (non-generic) small spherical perturbations. See also a
further extension of [12] by Singh-Zheng [13] and Zheng [14].
The present paper can be viewed as a non-spherically symmetric stability result and
the nonlinear singularity analysis of Christodoulou’s naked-singularity geometry beyond
spherical symmetry. In this paper, the scalar field enters as the leading terms in the analysis:
it supplies the singular background, enters the Ricci tensor through Ricµν = DµϕDνϕ,
modifies the Bianchi equations, creates a derivative-loss difficulty at the top order, and
ultimately yields the quantitative obstruction to extending the solution across the inner
Cauchy horizon.
Thus, this paper both advances the study of naked singularities and
establishes a precise nonlinear description of the singular inner Cauchy horizon beyond
spherical symmetry.
Besides overcoming the new difficulties arising from the matter field, compared with [11]
we additionally derive the precise asymptotic behavior of the solution as it approaches the
singular inner Cauchy horizon and establish the C1,
κ
1−κ+-inextendibility of the spacetime.
This hence links to
Conjecture 1.2 (Strong Cosmic Censorship, [6]). For generic initial data for suitable
Einstein-matter system or for Einstein vacuum equations, the maximal Cauchy develop-
ment arising from initial data is inextendible.
1.1. Definitions and Main Result. To rigorously state our results, we first introduce
the geometric framework of the double null foliation and the precise definition of the naked
singularity structure.

Definition 1.1 (Double Null Foliation). We introduce a double null foliation on the
(3 + 1)-dimensional spacetime (M, g). Consider a spacelike 2-sphere S−1,0, and let u and
v be future-directed optical functions (which satisfy the eikonal equation):
gµν∂µu∂νu = gµν∂µv∂νv = 0,
subject to the initial conditions u|S−1,0 = −1 and v|S−1,0 = 0.
We define the null geodesic vector fields L′ = −2∇u♯and L′ = −2∇v♯.
The lapse
function Ωis defined by the relation 2Ω−2 = −g(L′, L′). We define the normalized null
frame as e3 = ΩL′ and e4 = ΩL′.
Let Hu and Hv denote the level sets of u and v, respectively, where Hu corresponds to
outgoing null cones and Hv to incoming null cones. The intersection Su,v := Hu ∩Hv is a
topological 2-sphere.
We construct the coordinate system (u, v, θA) as follows: let (θA) denote arbitrary co-
ordinates on S−1,0, and propagate them throughout the spacetime by imposing the gauge
condition [e4, ∂θA] = 0. Let bA be the shift vector defined by e3 = Ω−1(∂u +bA∂A). In these
coordinates, the metric takes the form:
g = −2Ω2(u, v, θ)(du ⊗dv + dv ⊗du) + /gAB(u, v, θ)(dθA −bAdu) ⊗(dθB −bBdu). (1.3)
The associated null frame is given by e3 = Ω−1(∂u + bA∂A), e4 = Ω−1∂v, and eA = ∂θA for
A = 1, 2.
Definition 1.2 (Incomplete Future Null Infinity and Naked Singularity). Suppose that,
for a fixed u0, the outgoing null hypersurface Hu0 extends to an asymptotically flat region.
The spacetime (M, g) is said to possess an incomplete future null infinity if there exists
a sequence of points {pi} ⊂Hu0 with v(pi) →∞such that the future-directed incoming
null geodesics emanating from pi and generated by e3 have uniformly bounded affine length.
A spacetime is said to contain a naked singularity if it arises as the maximal globally hy-
perbolic development of regular initial data and possesses an incomplete future null infinity
I+.
We denote Christodoulou’s spherically symmetric, κ-self-similar background solution by
(Mc, gc, ϕc), which satisfies the symmetry conditions in (1.2). We are now ready to state
our main theorem.
Theorem 1.3. Let 0 < κ < 1/3 be fixed and δ > 0 be a small parameter. For any δ > 0,
there exists a sufficiently small ϵ = ϵ(δ) > 0 such that the following holds:
There exists a spacetime (M, g, ϕ) solving the Einstein-scalar field equations (1.1) with
the following properties:
(1) Global Structure: The manifold M is diffeomorphic to [−1, 0) × [0, ∞) × S2 with
coordinates (u, v, θ). The metric g takes the double null form described in Definition
1.1. In these coordinates, the metric of Christodoulou’s solution [4] is written by
gc = −2(Ωc)2(du ⊗dv + dv ⊗du) + /gc
ABdθA ⊗dθB.

spherically symmetric κ-self similar initial data as described in Section 3.1 and
along the initial outgoing null hypersurface u = −1 we impose small perturbations
as specified in Section 3.2. In particular, along v = 0 the initial data satisfy the
Lie-scaling condition uL∂ug = 2g, the quantity u∂uϕ +

an error of order O(ϵ), and the deviation of the metric from the background obeys

From the perspective of cosmic censorship, the above theorem has two complementary
features. First, future null infinity of the solution is incomplete in the sense of Definition
1.2; hence the singularity is visible from null infinity within the constructed class of data.
Second, the inner Cauchy horizon is itself singular. Thus, the same solutions exhibit both a
violation of weak cosmic censorship in its strict formulation and a manifestation of strong
cosmic censorship at the inner Cauchy horizon.
The regularity threshold is dictated by the self-similar background. The precise Hölder
regularity C1,
κ
1−κ arises from Christodoulou’s spherically symmetric solution, while the
detailed analysis in this paper yields a quantitative obstruction at the level C1, κ+δ
1−κ , for
every sufficiently small δ > 0, under non-spherically symmetric perturbations prescribed
on both the initial incoming and outgoing null hypersurfaces. This regularity threshold
is hence desired in the present non-spherically symmetric setting and is summarized as a
C1,
κ
1−κ+ inextendibility at the inner Cauchy horizon for the coupled Einstein-scalar field
system.

coordinates v/|u|—which reflects the self-similar nature of the background flow—we parti-

The overarching logic of the proof involves constructing an approximating solution via
Lie transport along the homothetic vector field S. We then estimate various differences,
including the difference between the actual solution and the approximations, using a system
of scale-invariant weighted norms. This allows us to propagate regularity from the initial
data surfaces (Region I) through the wave zone (Region II) and finally establish the singular
asymptotics at the horizon (Region III).
1.2.1. Lie Propagation Equation and the Approximating Solution. A primary challenge in
Region I lies in constructing an approximating solution that is sufficiently close to the
exact solution, thereby ensuring that the error terms remain controllable with respect to
both the perturbation size ϵ and the distance parameter z. The underlying self-similar
symmetry plays an indispensable role in this construction.

1.2.2. Gauge Choice and the Scale-Invariant Weighted Norm System. In the standard dou-
ble null gauge (u, v), the background solution exhibits singular scaling behavior near the
origin. Specifically, expressed in terms of the self-similar variable z = v/(−u), the back-
ground conformal factor behaves as (Ωc)2 ∼zκ as z →0, while the connection coefficient
scales according to (Ω−1ω)c ∼v−(1−κ)(−u)−κ.
To impose a consistent functional framework where the Ricci coefficients depend primar-
ily on the time coordinate, we introduce a renormalized coordinate system. Near the initial
cone (z ≈0, Region RI), we employ the transformation (ˆu, ˆv) = (u, v1−κ). Symmetrically,
away from z = 0 (Regions RII and RIII), we utilize (U, V ) = (−(−u)1−κ, v). For nota-
tional simplicity, within the analysis of Region RI, we will continue to use (u, v) to refer
to the renormalized gauge.
To close the energy estimates and cancel borderline terms arising from the background
geometry, we introduce a system of weighted norms.
Since the background quantities
scale as powers of −u or v along characteristics, we assign a specific weight—termed the
signature—to each geometric quantity. The signature s(ψ) represents the expected decay
rate of the L2 norm of a tensor ψ.
In Region RI = {0 < z < ϵ1, −1 < u < 0}, we define a modified L2 norm over the
2-spheres Su,v by approximating the geometric volume form dVolg with (−u)2dVS2. We
incorporate an additional weight function w(u, v) to adequately handle the fine structure
of the error terms. For example, since the background Gaussian curvature evaluates to
∥Kc∥L2(Su,v) ∼(−u)−1, we assign the geometric signature s(K) = −1. The corresponding
spatial norms take the form:

Comparison with the Vacuum Case. A critical distinction between this work and the
vacuum result in [11] lies in the regularity requirements. In the vacuum case, one can rely
on the simpler difference ψ −ψ|v=0. However, for the Einstein-scalar field system (1.1), the
curvature estimates at order n depend on the n + 1-th derivatives of the scalar field (via
terms like DµϕDνϕ). Consequently, we must control the n + 1-th order energy estimates
for the Ricci coefficients. More specifically, we use ψ to represent the first order derivatives
related to g, Ω, ϕ and Ψ the curvature components. The equations of Einstein vacuum
systems are simply written as
∇3Ψ1 + DΨ2 ∼ψΨ, ∇4Ψ2 −∗DΨ1 ∼ψΨ,
where D is a differencial operator with Hodge dual ∗D.
However, within the Einstein
scalar-field system, the right hand sides of these equations will be:
∇3Ψ1 + DΨ2 ∼ψΨ + ψ∇ψ, ∇4Ψ2 −∗DΨ1 ∼ψΨ + ψ∇ψ.
This loss-of-derivative structure necessitates the finer control provided by our hierarchy of

1.2.4. Asymptotic Analysis in Region III: Renormalization and Bulk Integration. Region
RIII presents the most significant analytic challenge. As the solution approaches the inner
Cauchy horizon (z →∞), the curvature component α is expected to blow up. Unlike in
Region I, we cannot subtract a smooth approximating solution
△α because the background
itself becomes singular. Consequently, we cannot estimate the absolute difference directly.
To resolve this, we employ a strategy of renormalization. We assign specific weights
Ωt(ψ) to each Ricci coefficient ψ and curvature component Ψ (excluding the transversal
components ω and α) to neutralize their expected blow-up rates. In Section 7, we prove
that the renormalized difference decays as:

1.4. Outline of the Paper. The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
Section 2 fixes the double null formalism for the Einstein-scalar field equations. We
record the null structure equations, the renormalized Bianchi equations, the relevant prop-
erties of Christodoulou’s self-similar background in the double null gauge, and the charac-
teristic local existence theorem used to start the construction.
Section 3 constructs the characteristic initial data. We solve the degenerate Lie prop-
agation equations on the initial incoming cone, construct the approximating shear and
scalar-field derivative along the outgoing cone, and prescribe data that remain close to the
Christodoulou background while allowing non-spherical perturbations.
Section 4 develops the weighted functional framework. We introduce the regional gauges,
the scale-invariant signatures, the weighted sphere, null, and bulk norms, and the Sobolev,
transport, and elliptic estimates used throughout the proof.
Section 5 carries out the Region I analysis near the initial cone. This section proves
the weighted estimates for the four-type difference hierarchy, including the second-order
subtraction for trχ, and culminates in the interface estimates on the fixed self-similar
boundary.
Section 6 treats Region II. Using the Region I interface data, we prove high-order stability
estimates around the Christodoulou background with the V -signature norm system and
the arbitrary factor V a. The section also contains the top-order scalar-field estimates, the
auxiliary treatment of Ωω and Ω−1ω, and the renormalized mass-aspect estimates needed
to recover the Ricci coefficients.


## Page 12


12
XINLIANG AN AND SHENGRONG WU
Section 7 proves the Region III estimates near the inner Cauchy horizon. The analysis
uses weighted background-renormalized differences and spacetime-integrated bulk norms
to close the Bianchi and scalar-field estimates in the singular asymptotic region.
Section 8 identifies the limiting causal and regularity structure. We prove the affine-
length estimate giving the incompleteness of future null infinity and derive the blow-up of
the incoming transverse second derivative of the scalar field. This yields the singular inner
Cauchy horizon and the C1, κ+δ
1−κ inextendibility statement.

2.2. Christodoulou’s Naked Singularity. The background spacetime for our pertur-
bation analysis is the spherically symmetric, continuously self-similar naked singularity
solution constructed by Christodoulou in his seminal work [4]. This construction effec-
tively reduces the Einstein-scalar field equations to a system of ordinary differential equa-
tions with respect to the self-similar variable, which are subsequently analyzed employing
standard dynamical systems techniques.
A spherically symmetric configuration is described by the triple (r, m, ϕ), where r is the
area radius, m is the Hawking mass, and ϕ is the scalar field. The system admits a scaling
symmetry under the group R+ × R, where an element (a, b) maps a solution (r, m, ϕ) to
(ar, am, ϕ + b). A solution is said to be **k-continuously self-similar (k-CSS)** if there
exists a homothetic Killing vector field S such that the spacetime is invariant under the
one-parameter diffeomorphism group Φa generated by S, up to a shift in the scalar field:
Φ∗
a(g) = a2g,
Φ∗
a(ϕ) = ϕ +
√
2κ log a.

Although the original construction in [4] employed self-similar Bondi coordinates, we adapt
this exact solution to the double null gauge to facilitate our stability analysis. We sum-
marize the key analytic properties of this background solution relevant to our proof, and

Theorem 2.1 (Properties of the Background Solution). For any fixed κ ∈(0, 1/3), there
exists an asymptotically flat, spherically symmetric solution (Mc, gc, ϕc) to the Einstein-
scalar field equations in the causal domain Q = {u < 0, v > u}, satisfying the following
properties:
(1) Global Structure: The spacetime admits a global double null coordinate system
satisfying the symmetry condition (2.13).
(2) Regularity: The scalar function ϕ and the conformal factor Ω2 are smooth in the
interior Q \ {v = 0}. Near the boundaries {v = 0} and {u = 0}, the solution
belongs to the Hölder class C1,

2.3. The Characteristic Initial Value Problem. We now review the local well-posedness
of the characteristic initial value problem (CIVP) for the Einstein-scalar field system. For
the Einstein vacuum equations, interested readers are referred to the work [8] by Luk,
which is an extension of Rendall’s result [10] with harmonic gauge. Here, we formulate the
analogous result for the coupled Einstein-scalar field system.
The underlying strategy relies on reducing the full system to a set of quasilinear wave
equations formulated in harmonic coordinates. We cite the relevant local existence theorem
due to Rendall:
Theorem 2.2 (Rendall [10]). Consider a quasilinear wave equation of the form
gµν(ϕ)∂2

3. Initial Values and Approximating Solution
In this section, we construct exactly κ-self-similar initial data along the incoming null
hypersurface H0, and approximately self-similar initial data along the outgoing null hyper-
surface H−1.
3.1. Construction of Self-Similar Initial Data on H0. We begin by constructing the
data along v = 0 to strictly satisfy the κ-self-similarity condition. Suppose a spacetime
(M, g) admits a homothetic Killing field S = u∂u + v∂v satisfying the κ-self-similarity

In this article, we rigorously prove the global existence result formulated as follows.
Theorem 3.11 (Global Existence). The spacetime (M, g, ϕ) associated with the character-
istic initial data (3.45) admits global a priori energy estimates. Consequently, the spacetime
can be globally developed over the entire exterior characteristic initial region −1 < u < 0
and 0 < v < ∞, revealing a globally naked singularity located at u = 0, v = 0.
4. Weighted Scale Invariant Norms and Useful Lemmas
4.1. Weighted norm system. We first define signatures and a scale-invariant weighted
norm system to simplify the calculation in later sections.
Definition 4.1. We define the signature for the geometric quantities of interest:
s(Ω2) = κ + 1,
s(b) = s(g) = s(ϕ) = 1,
s(Ω−1trχ) = s(Ωtrχ) = s(Ω−1 ˆχ) = s(Ω−1ω) = s(Ωω) = s(η) = s(η) = 0,
s(Ω2α) = s(Ω−2α) = s(Ω−1β) = s(Ωβ) = s(ρ) = s(σ) = −1.
Furthermore, we establish the following conventions for derivatives and contractions:
s(∇) = s(ΩD3) = s(Ω−1D4) = 0,
s(Φ1Φ2) = s(Φ1) + s(Φ2) −1,

Remark 8. Heuristically, we expect the metric to remain close to the spherically sym-
metric and exactly self-similar background metric found in [4]. Therefore, · serves as the
appropriate basic difference, which will be primarily utilized in Regions II and III. In Re-
gion I, however, we require more accurate difference quantities, which are expected to satisfy
bounds of the form ϵ

v
(−u)1−κ
C
for suitable constants C. Consequently, we introduce [·]v
0
and {·}v
0 to carefully isolate the remaining terms corresponding to the zeroth and first-order
Taylor expansions. Nevertheless, the leading terms Ω−1 ˆχ, Ω−1e4ϕ, and Ω−2α cannot be
effectively estimated using these basic differences alone; we thus approximate them explic-
itly by solving their evolution equations. The three difference notations should be read as a
hierarchy. The symbol e· is used after an explicit model term has been subtracted, [·]v
0 records
a zeroth-order increment away from v = 0, and {·}v
0 records the remainder after the first
v-Taylor term has also been removed. This hierarchy explains why later product estimates
always separate explicit background increments from the genuinely small difference terms.
For these differences, we state the formulae to compute difference of product. They can
be proved by direct calculation.

These two immediate consequences of the bootstrap assumptions will be used silently in
the estimates below: they provide uniform control for all non-difference factors, while
the weighted bootstrap bounds provide the small factor attached to whichever factor is a
difference. Whenever a product is estimated, the relevant product lemma identifies which
factor carries the difference and the uniform bound above controls the remaining factors.

trivial weight W = 1 by L1. Throughout this specific section, we will primarily focus on
analyzing the fundamental difference quantities Φ = Φ −Φc.

small, such that the bootstrap constant in (6.4) can be strictly improved to B ≤1.

cones produces a spacetime estimate with an arbitrarily small prefactor once D is chosen
sufficiently large. This small prefactor is the key tool used in the Region II bootstrap
closure.

Remark 17. We use additional notation for those with enhanced estimates in (7.38):
φ1 ∈{Ωχ, Ω−1trχ, η, Ωω, Ωe4ϕ, ∇ϕ},
and for all connection components and first order derivatives of scalar field:
φ ∈{ψ1, ψ2, ∇ϕ, Ω−1e3ϕ, Ωe4ϕ}.
In the whole section, we will avoid Ω−1ω and no notation refers to it. The three bootstrap
assumptions are hierarchical. The first controls flux and ordinary spacetime norms, the
second gives stronger spherical control for quantities with favorable ∇3 equations, and the
third records the weaker but still sufficient control for the incoming components Ω−1ˆχ and
Ω−1e3ϕ.

8. Asymptotic Analysis near Inner Cauchy Horizon
This final section extracts the geometric consequences of the estimates proved in the
three regions. The first point is that the constructed spacetime reaches the limiting inner
boundary in finite affine time, so the singularity is visible from the regular region. The
second point is more precise: the scalar field develops a transverse blow-up at the inner
Cauchy horizon strong enough to obstruct the expected higher regularity extensions.
Combining the local existence results and the a priori estimates, we establish the follow-
ing proposition.
Proposition 8.1. With the initial data prescribed as (3.45), the spacetime (M, g, ϕ) can
be solved globally and contains a naked singularity.

Appendix A. Derivation of equations
This appendix records the structural identities used earlier: the self-similar system on
the initial hypersurface, the difference equation for Ωtrχ, and the transport identities for
the scalar and torsion quantities.

[1] Xinliang An. Naked singularity censoring with anisotropic apparent horizon. Annals of Mathematics,
201(3):775–908, 2025.

field equations. Communications on Pure and Applied Mathematics, 46(8):1131–1220, 1993.
[4] Demetrios Christodoulou. Examples of naked singularity formation in the gravitational collapse of a
scalar field. Annals of Mathematics, 140(3):607–653, 1994.
[5] Demetrios Christodoulou. The instability of naked singularities in the gravitational collapse of a scalar
field. Annals of Mathematics, 149(1):183–217, 1999.
[6] Demetrios Christodoulou. On the global initial value problem and the issue of singularities. Classical
and Quantum Gravity, 16(12A):A23–A35, 1999.
[7] Junbin Li and Jue Liu. Instability of spherical naked singularities of a scalar field under gravitational
perturbations. Journal of Differential Geometry, 120(1):97–197, 2022.
[8] Jonathan Luk. On the local existence for the characteristic initial value problem in general relativity.
International Mathematics Research Notices, 2012(20):4625–4678, 2012.
[9] Roger Penrose. Gravitational collapse: the role of general relativity. Rivista del Nuovo Cimento, 1:252–
276, 1969.

its applications to the Einstein equations. Proceedings of the Royal Society of London. Series A:

tions: the exterior solution. Annals of Mathematics, 198(1):231–391, 2023.

symmetric Einstein-scalar field system. Annales Henri Poincaré, 26:2355–2465, 2025.
